That was about two years and a couple months ago now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.

But the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.

So yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m just tired all the time. I mean, I teach, so being wiped out at the end of the day is kind of part of the job—especially with kids needing pencils every five seconds and the copier always jamming—but this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m just tired all the time. I mean, I teach, so being wiped out at the end of the day is kind of part of the job—especially with kids needing pencils every five seconds and the copier always jamming—but this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 35 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, but underneath it feels like there’s this gray, flat feeling that follows me home and makes even my garden or watercolors seem far away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.

I mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.

So… not really getting suddenly worse or better. Just quietly there, most of the time.

At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic “I hate everything” way, because I can still go to work and smile at my students, and honestly some of them say funny things that make me laugh for a second.

Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m just tired all the time. I mean, I teach, so being wiped out at the end of the day is kind of part of the job—especially with kids needing pencils every five seconds and the copier always jamming—but this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.\n\nSo… not really getting suddenly worse or better. Just quietly there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic “I hate everything” way, because I can still go to work and smile at my students, and honestly some of them say funny things that make me laugh for a second.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling just comes in with me. I’ll see the garden—there are probably weeds everywhere by now, and I used to be so particular about the herbs—and my watercolors will be sitting out, and I know I used to enjoy those things. They just feel… distant. Like I can remember liking them more than I can actually feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Um… weight? I don’t think so. Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit about the same, I think. Though I did have this one skirt I stopped wearing, but that was more because the zipper annoys me, not because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… um. That’s a big question.

I don’t really know how to answer it neatly. I’ve definitely had days where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I wish I could put my life on mute, if that makes sense. It’s kind of like when the classroom gets too loud and you turn the lights off for a minute so everyone settles—sorry, that’s not really the same thing.

But ending my life… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve sat down and made some kind of plan or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired. I get really tired of feeling this way.

Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.\n\nSo… not really getting suddenly worse or better. Just quietly there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic “I hate everything” way, because I can still go to work and smile at my students, and honestly some of them say funny things that make me laugh for a second.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling just comes in with me. I’ll see the garden—there are probably weeds everywhere by now, and I used to be so particular about the herbs—and my watercolors will be sitting out, and I know I used to enjoy those things. They just feel… distant. Like I can remember liking them more than I can actually feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Um… weight? I don’t think so. Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit about the same, I think. Though I did have this one skirt I stopped wearing, but that was more because the zipper annoys me, not because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… um. That’s a big question.\n\nI don’t really know how to answer it neatly. I’ve definitely had days where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I wish I could put my life on mute, if that makes sense. Like I can remember liking them more than I can actually feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Um… weight? I don’t think so. Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit about the same, I think. Though I did have this one skirt I stopped wearing, but that was more because the zipper annoys me, not because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… um. That’s a big question.\n\nI don’t really know how to answer it neatly. I’ve definitely had days where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I wish I could put my life on mute, if that makes sense. It’s kind of like when the classroom gets too loud and you turn the lights off for a minute so everyone settles—sorry, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nBut ending my life… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve sat down and made some kind of plan or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired. I get really tired of feeling this way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you no longer find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, such as your garden or watercolors?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 35 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, but underneath it feels like there’s this gray, flat feeling that follows me home and makes even my garden or watercolors seem far away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… I think that’s still true. I can get through the day at school, and I smile at the kids, and sometimes it’s real for a moment—like when one of them says something completely off the wall and I have to laugh.

But underneath, most days, there’s just this grayness. And then I come home and the garden is there, or my watercolors are sitting out, and it’s like… I know those things used to feel good. I know they mattered to me. They just feel kind of far away now, like I can see them but can’t quite reach the part of me that enjoyed them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m just tired all the time. I mean, I teach, so being wiped out at the end of the day is kind of part of the job—especially with kids needing pencils every five seconds and the copier always jamming—but this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.\n\nSo… not really getting suddenly worse or better. Just quietly there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic “I hate everything” way, because I can still go to work and smile at my students, and honestly some of them say funny things that make me laugh for a second.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling just comes in with me. I’ll see the garden—there are probably weeds everywhere by now, and I used to be so particular about the herbs—and my watercolors will be sitting out, and I know I used to enjoy those things. They just feel… distant. Like I can remember liking them more than I can actually feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Um… weight? I don’t think so. Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit about the same, I think. Though I did have this one skirt I stopped wearing, but that was more because the zipper annoys me, not because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… um. That’s a big question.\n\nI don’t really know how to answer it neatly. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things I have to do, like at school I can smile and get through the lessons, and sometimes the kids are genuinely funny—yesterday one of them asked if clouds get tired, which… anyway.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m just tired all the time. I mean, I teach, so being wiped out at the end of the day is kind of part of the job—especially with kids needing pencils every five seconds and the copier always jamming—but this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.\n\nSo… not really getting suddenly worse or better. Just quietly there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic “I hate everything” way, because I can still go to work and smile at my students, and honestly some of them say funny things that make me laugh for a second.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling just comes in with me. I’ll see the garden—there are probably weeds everywhere by now, and I used to be so particular about the herbs—and my watercolors will be sitting out, and I know I used to enjoy those things. Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit about the same, I think. Yeah… yes, I think so. More than two weeks, honestly. Most days I can still do what I’m supposed to do—I get through lessons, I smile at my students, I mean you kind of have to when you’re standing in front of a room full of kids asking if worms have feelings.

But underneath all of that, it’s just this gray, flat feeling. And then I go home and it follows me there. The garden is there, my watercolors are there, and it’s like I remember they used to matter to me, but they feel really far away. Like I can see them, but I can’t quite get back to the part of myself that enjoyed them.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m just tired all the time. I mean, I teach, so being wiped out at the end of the day is kind of part of the job—especially with kids needing pencils every five seconds and the copier always jamming—but this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I still have to really push myself to get through grading, making dinner, and getting everything ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Sort of, but not really the way I used to. At school I can still put on a smile for the kids and get through the lessons—sometimes they’ll say something funny and I’ll laugh, and then I’m back to, you know, making sure everyone has their folders and nobody’s eating glue.\n\nBut underneath it, most days, there’s this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. I used to really like working in my garden or doing little watercolor things at the kitchen table, and now they just feel… far away, I guess. Like I can see that I used to enjoy them, but I can’t quite get to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and maybe two months. It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine shifted.\n\nI remember because suddenly mornings felt different—like where things were kept in the kitchen, who was doing what, even silly things like the lunch containers being in the wrong drawer—and I kept thinking I’d adjust once we got into a new rhythm. But that gray feeling just kind of stayed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I still have moments with my students where I smile—like if one of them says something completely off-the-wall during a lesson, I do laugh. Kids are funny that way, even when they’re also, you know, somehow losing every single glue stick in the room.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s still this gray, flat feeling. And when I go home, things I used to look forward to—like my garden or doing watercolors at the kitchen table—just feel kind of far away. Like I know I used to enjoy them, but I don’t really reach for them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, besides the occasional laugh with your students?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can still function—I still smile at school and get through the lessons, and sometimes I even seem like myself there. But when I get home, it’s like that gray, flat feeling settles back in.\n\nI’ll see my garden out the window and think, oh, I should go check on that, or I’ll notice my watercolors sitting there, and then somehow it just feels too far away. Not like I hate them, exactly… more like the part of me that used to look forward to them is hard to reach.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt better, where this gray, flat feeling lifted for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I keep thinking there must have been a better week in there somewhere, like maybe during a school break or when the house was quieter, but I don’t remember it actually lifting.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been there the whole time, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and everything at home kind of changed. That was about two years and a couple months ago now. The routine shifted, and I kept thinking once we got used to it I’d feel normal again, but… I don’t think I really did.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I worry about normal things—school stuff, whether I remembered to send a note home, that kind of thing. There was this one parent email last week that I kept rereading because the tone felt… I don’t know, sharp? But then sometimes emails just sound worse than they are.\n\nBut “often anxious,” I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that being the main thing. It’s more like… I’d have to think about what you mean by worried, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still doing the things I have to do. I show up for my students, I make dinner or help at home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut the extra parts of life… those have sort of fallen away. I used to say yes to mentoring newer teachers, or meeting people after work, or making time for little creative routines. And now I either don’t go, or I put it off until it’s too late. It’s funny, there’s this group of teachers who always invite me for coffee on Fridays, and I keep meaning to go, but then I think about parking, and small talk, and whether I’ll have anything interesting to say, and I just go home.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest—not completely in a dramatic way, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons. They probably wouldn’t know anything was wrong most days.\n\nBut underneath it’s like this gray, flat feeling that just kind of stays with me. I’ll come home and see the garden needs attention, or my watercolors are sitting there, and it’s like they’re far away somehow. Like I remember I used to care about them more, but I can’t quite get to that feeling. So… yes, most days, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I’d say mild… I think. It’s not like I can’t function. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.\n\nSo… not really getting suddenly worse or better. Just quietly there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic “I hate everything” way, because I can still go to work and smile at my students, and honestly some of them say funny things that make me laugh for a second.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling just comes in with me. I’ll see the garden—there are probably weeds everywhere by now, and I used to be so particular about the herbs—and my watercolors will be sitting out, and I know I used to enjoy those things. They just feel… distant. Like I can remember liking them more than I can actually feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Um… weight? I don’t think so. Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit about the same, I think. Though I did have this one skirt I stopped wearing, but that was more because the zipper annoys me, not because—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… um. That’s a big question.\n\nI don’t really know how to answer it neatly. I’ve definitely had days where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I wish I could put my life on mute, if that makes sense. It’s kind of like when the classroom gets too loud and you turn the lights off for a minute so everyone settles—sorry, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nBut ending my life… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve sat down and made some kind of plan or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired. I get really tired of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, such as your garden or watercolors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I think that’s still true. I can get through the day at school, and I smile at the kids, and sometimes it’s real for a moment—like when one of them says something completely off the wall and I have to laugh.\n\nBut underneath, most days, there’s just this grayness. And then I come home and the garden is there, or my watercolors are sitting out, and it’s like… I know those things used to feel good. I know they mattered to me. They just feel kind of far away now, like I can see them but can’t quite reach the part of me that enjoyed them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still smile for my students, I get through the lessons, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut then I get home and it’s like that gray, flat feeling has followed me there. I’ll see the garden or my watercolors, and I know those are things I used to feel connected to, but they just seem kind of far away. So… mild, but pretty constant, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has the intensity of this gray, flat feeling changed at all over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s changed in a big, obvious way. It’s more like… it’s just been there, kind of steady.\n\nI mean, most days I can still smile for my students and get through my lessons, so part of me feels silly calling it intense. But then I go home and it’s like the same gray, flat feeling has followed me in the door. I’ll look at the garden or my watercolors, and they’re right there, but they feel sort of far away from me.\n\nSo… not really getting suddenly worse or better. I’ll see the garden—there are probably weeds everywhere by now, and I used to be so particular about the herbs—and my watercolors will be sitting out, and I know I used to enjoy those things. And then I come home and the garden is there, or my watercolors are sitting out, and it’s like… I know those things used to feel good. I know they mattered to me. They just feel kind of far away now, like I can see them but can’t quite reach the part of me that enjoyed them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m not sure I’d say “no longer enjoy” most of the day exactly… it’s hard to tell. I mean, when I’m at school, the day sort of has its own momentum—bells, worksheets, someone needing a pencil every four seconds. Yesterday one of my students tried to convince me his eraser was “emotionally unavailable,” which was… honestly pretty funny.

But enjoyment is harder to pin down. I know some things used to feel easier to enjoy, like they’d pull me in more naturally. Now I notice I’m kind of watching myself do them, or thinking about doing them, instead of really feeling much. So… maybe? I don’t know if that answers it.

I mean, “really down” sounds a little dramatic, because I can still smile at my students and get through the lessons.
